After stacks of paperwork and coordination, the MPs remanded me to the barracks under temporary confinement. Fortunately, I managed to convince the sergeant on duty that I posed zero flight risk, securing a two-hour pass to rush over and salvage the date with Terri.

When I formally stood before my Company Commander to plead my case, my time-stamped receipt from the initial purchase backed up my timeline. The Captain recognized genuine small-town Ozark naivete when he saw it. He issued a firm administrative reprimand, dismissed the formal charges, and expunged my record completely.

It was a sharp, memorable wake-up call in institutional bureaucracy: in the wider world, good intentions don't substitute for clear documentation, and you'd better cross every 't' and dot every 'i' before making an exchange.

* **Core Heuristic & Persona Takeaway:**
  * **Institutional Awareness & Process Rigor:** Unwritten community customs do not translate to structured bureaucratic systems; always follow explicit protocol and secure formal documentation.
  * **Salvaging Crises with Candid Truth:** When caught in an absurd misunderstanding, communicate transparently and act immediately to rebuild trust.

---

### Story 5.12: The Baldie Herd and the 13% CDs — Agricultural Leverage, Drought Arbitrage, and Total Debt Freedom at 32 (1972–1980)

* **Search Triggers / Alternate Titles:** Cow-calf operation expansion, 17 cows to 43 black baldies, agricultural leverage home financing, cattle liquidation 1980, 220-acre hay harvest, 1980 Ozarks drought, emergency feed subsidy arbitrage, 13 percent bank CDs, total debt freedom at 32, retiring at 52, net-zero mortgage, building equity without consumer debt.
* **Key People:** Terri (wife & partner), Dad (financial philosophy mentor), Hereford bull.
* **Visual Artifact / Photo Link:** *(None cataloged)*

While Terri and I were swinging hammers, hanging drywall, and hoisting rafters on the solar house, we were running a parallel second shift out in the pastures. In the summer of 1972, we had seeded our agricultural venture by purchasing seventeen pregnant cows. Over the next eight years, through freezing Ozark winters, muddy calving seasons, and relentless pasture management, we systematically scaled the herd. By the spring of 1980, the operation had grown into forty-three prime black baldie brood cows, a purebred Hereford herd bull, forty heavy calves primed for the auction block, and a fresh scattering of newborns hitting the turf.

Our lifestyle during that window was monastic by design. Teaching full-time, building a multi-story home with our own hands, and working a commercial herd meant we were simply too busy to spend disposable income. We lived on pennies, pouring every dollar above our baseline survival expenses straight back into materials and working capital. Instead of shackling our future to a conventional thirty-year bank mortgage, we engineered a calculated agricultural hedge: we used short-term livestock production loans secured against the herd to cash-flow the building supplies, lumber bunks, and livestock expansion simultaneously. The cattle were physically financing the roof over our heads.

The master stroke arrived in the spring of 1980 when commodity timing and Mother Nature aligned in our favor. Cattle markets hit a cyclically high pricing peak, and we recognized the window. We didn't get greedy; we loaded up and liquidated the entire operation in one sweep—brood cows, herd bull, and the calf crop. We took the gross sale proceeds and completely eradicated every cent of livestock and construction debt.

Right as the herd cleared out, that season’s spring rains yielded an exceptional clover and fescue crop across our rented 220-acre farm. I spent weeks in the dust, cutting, raking, and baling until the barn rafters were packed to the eaves with top-grade square bales. Then the skies shut down.

The summer of 1980 delivered one of the most brutal, scorching droughts in modern Ozarks history. Pastures burned to brown crisps, ponds dried to cracked mud, and desperate livestock producers across the county faced catastrophic herd starvation. The federal government stepped in with emergency livestock feed subsidies to keep regional herds alive, and suddenly, my dry barn full of prime cured hay became liquid gold. I sold the entire harvest at drought-scarcity peak pricing.

We took the hay windfall and immediately locked it into high-yield bank Certificates of Deposit, which in the inflation-fighting climate of 1980 were paying an astronomical 13% interest compounded quarterly.

By the summer of 1980, at thirty-two years of age, Terri and I stood completely debt-free. We owned a hand-built, 3,500-square-foot, four-bedroom, three-bath passive solar home with zero mortgage, a fully equipped workshop, and a compounding cash cushion generating guaranteed double-digit yields.

It wasn't a mechanical application of Dad's childhood 90/10 coin-jar rule, but it was born of the exact same marrow: refuse consumer pretense, build sweat equity, respect liquidity, and seize market dislocations when they present themselves. In subsequent decades, whenever we leveraged debt to acquire rental houses, we maintained an absolute balance-sheet rule: never owe a lender a dollar unless you already hold significantly more cash sitting safely in the vault. That discipline broke the wage trap, insulated us against market panics, and gave us the sovereign freedom to walk away from public education at age fifty-two to enjoy the good life on our own terms.

* **Core Heuristic & Persona Takeaway:**
  * **Asymmetric Asset Leverage:** Borrowing against productive, reproducing biological assets (a breeding herd) to build physical equity (a debt-free home) beats dead-weight consumer amortizations every single day.
  * **Commodity Discipline & Scarcity Arbitrage:** Recognize cyclic tops and cash out cleanly; stockpiled physical commodities (like hay before a drought) transform into explosive liquidity when nature and policy intersect.
  * **The Sovereignty Threshold:** Total debt freedom achieved early in life compounds far beyond interest rates—it buys psychological autonomy, allows strategic risk in real estate, and sets the stage for an early, deliberate retirement.

---

### Story 5.13: Five-Foot-One Fury and the Sears Mustard Paint — Grandma Hattie Martin and the Posey Lineage (c. 1960s–1970s)

* **Search Triggers / Alternate Titles:** Hattie Martin grandmother, five foot one bundle of fury, Barry Posey Korea deployment, screen scratching window peeper, broomstick backyard charge, Peggy Sears paint standoff, mustard brown floor paint threat, Terri childhood bedroom Lawton OK.
* **Key People:** Hattie Martin (Terri's maternal grandmother), Peggy (Martin) Posey (Terri's mother), Barry Posey (Terri's father), Terri Evans (wife), Shari Posey.
* **Visual Artifact / Photo Link:** *(None cataloged)*

When I started dating Terri down in Oklahoma while stationed at Fort Sill, I quickly realized that the women in her family were forged from pure frontier grit. You didn't cross them, you didn't patronize them, and you certainly didn't try to intimidate them.

The matriarch of that household was Terri’s maternal grandmother, Hattie Martin. Standing barely five feet, one inch tall, Grandma Hattie was an absolute bundle of fury. She lived under the same roof with Terri, her parents Barry and Peggy, Terri's three growing brothers, and baby sister Shari. As the household expanded and the younger children needed space, Hattie was moved into Terri’s bedroom. By the time I met Terri around 1970, she was living in a shared student house near the University of Oklahoma campus in Norman, but whenever she returned to Lawton on weekends, she and Grandma Hattie were still sharing that same room.

Everyone in the Posey family had a favorite story about Hattie, and it dated back to when Terri was about ten years old and her father, Barry, was deployed overseas with the military in Korea. 

It was an ordinary evening in Lawton. Terri was in the bathroom taking an evening tub bath, while her mother Peggy sat at the kitchen table balancing the household budget in the quiet house. Through the bathroom door, Peggy heard repeated splashing sounds against the exterior wall and started shouting: "Teresa Dianne, you stop splashing that bathwater!"

Terri yelled back through the door: "Mama, I’m not splashing anything!"

A second later, Peggy glanced up from her checkbook toward the kitchen window and let out a blood-curdling scream. 

Pressed flat against the glass in the dark was the face of an unknown man, staring into the house and actively scratching his fingernails against the wire window screen. 

Most people in an isolated home with their husband stationed halfway across the globe in Korea would have locked themselves in a closet or panicked. Not Hattie Martin. 

Hearing Peggy's scream, Grandma Hattie didn't pause to call the authorities. She grabbed a heavy wooden broom from the corner, kicked the back screen door wide open, and charged into the pitch-black yard like an enraged badger, brandishing that broom over her head and screaming at the top of her lungs: "You better run off! Come here, you bastard, I’ll beat you senseless!" 

The prowler took one look at this five-foot-one tornado bearing down on him with a raised broomstick and ran for his life down the alley. Knowing Hattie’s temper and practical follow-through, it was an enormous blessing for the man that his legs carried him away in time.

That refusal to be pushed around was an inherited trait. When Terri was a teenager, she decided she wanted to paint her bedroom. Peggy drove her down to the local Sears store to purchase two gallons of interior paint. Terri carefully selected the exact shade she wanted—a soft pastel blue—and they paid the clerk, watched them mix the pigment on the shaker machine, and drove home with the sealed cans.

When they popped the lids in the bedroom, it wasn't pastel blue. It was a vile, swampy mustard-brown.

Peggy and Terri loaded the cans back into the car, drove back to the Sears paint counter, and explained the mechanical error. The store manager came over, looked at the receipt, and flatly refused to help, reciting rigid company bureaucracy: "Sears store policy is absolute: no refunds and no exchanges on custom-tinted paint."

Peggy looked him dead in the eye: "This was entirely your mistake. You mixed the wrong formula."

The manager shrugged: "Doesn't matter. Store policy is final."

Terri stood back against the counter, completely mortified and wishing the floor would open up to swallow her. But Peggy was done negotiating. She set the open gallon of mustard paint down on the counter, leaned in close, and said with icy, unblinking calm:

"Mister, you are either going to mix a brand-new batch of pastel blue paint for my daughter right now, or I am going to paint your showroom floor right here a lovely mustard brown—free of charge. And you won't like it."

When Peggy Posey dropped into that low, flat register, everyone who knew her understood that she wasn't bluffing for a second. The manager took one look at her face, saw that she was about two seconds away from dumping the gallon across his linoleum, decided his store policy wasn't worth the cleanup, and immediately mixed Terri two fresh gallons of blue. 

Fortunately for me, Terri inherited all of her family’s steadfast loyalty and moral courage without that volatile, hair-trigger aggression. But having Hattie Martin and Peggy Posey as living blueprints was an enduring reminder of where that family’s backbone was forged.

* **Core Heuristic & Persona Takeaway:**
  * **Asymmetric Defense & Absolute Resolve:** When a genuine threat breaches your domestic perimeter (like a prowler on the window screen), hesitation invites disaster. Immediate, aggressive counter-action—even a five-foot-one grandmother with a broom—disarms an aggressor through sheer shock of will.
  * **Calling Bureaucratic Bluffs:** Rigid corporate policies often crumble when confronted by calm, unyielding resolve; when right is on your side, making the cost of administrative obstinance higher than the cost of doing what is fair resolves the standoff.
  * **Inherited Tenacity:** Strong matriarchs forge resilient households; appreciating where a partner’s inner strength originated builds lifelong respect for their family line.

---

### Story 5.14: The 150-Day Early Out, Australian Bauxite, and the Arc Welder — Tradecraft and Structural Steel (1971–1972)**

* **Search Triggers / Alternate Titles:** Army early out for school, 150-day early release 1972, vocational welding certification Norman OK, Australian bauxite mine job offer, stick arc welder purchase, dual canoe trailers, pasture roller build, restored manure spreader, 20-foot solar rolling gate, structural steel fabrication.
* **Key People:** Terri Evans (wife), Gene Evans (Dad), Military Company Commander, Trade School Instructors.
* **Visual Artifact / Photo Link:** `![Rolling Powered gate](https://i.postimg.cc/QC8kbTB5/Rolling-Powered-gate.jpg)`

In the late summer of 1970, I had been swept into the Army under draft lottery number 15 for a mandatory two-year active duty hitch, which established my official Expiration of Term of Service (ETS) for late August 1972. 

By late 1971, however, national military logistics shifted dramatically. President Nixon was aggressively accelerating the Vietnam drawdown, and the Department of the Army was under strict congressional orders to slash troop ceilings. To reduce payrolls without paying out massive severance benefits, the military temporarily expanded its administrative early-release policy: draftees who could produce verified proof of acceptance to an accredited college or vocational trade program were permitted an early discharge of up to 150 to 180 days before their scheduled ETS date.

Having already earned my mathematics degree from SMS prior to induction, I had no immediate undergraduate courses to take. But Terri was in Norman finishing her secondary English degree at the University of Oklahoma, and we had set our wedding date for January 10, 1972. 

I applied the systemic leverage: I enrolled in an intensive, professional vocational welding certification program in Oklahoma. With my official enrollment papers submitted through the chain of command, the Army processed my early discharge under the school drawdown policy. In February 1972—roughly six months ahead of schedule—I walked off Fort Sill with an honorable discharge in hand.

Welding school was a masterclass in physical metallurgy, puddle control, and structural integrity. I spent hours under the hood mastering shielded metal arc welding (stick), oxyacetylene cutting, joint geometry, and overhead penetration, ultimately testing out to earn my certified welder credentials. 

Right before graduation, commercial recruiters visited the facility. Impressed with my technical test welds, they made an aggressive offer: a lucrative contract to work as a commercial welder in the bauxite mining operations of Western Australia. The compensation was massive compared to public school wages, but it carried an inflexible multi-year foreign commitment. I was an academic and an educator by temperament, and Terri and I were eager to return to our roots in the Ozarks to build our homestead and start our classroom careers; I turned Australia down flat without looking back.

The tradecraft itself, however, became one of the greatest practical force-multipliers of my entire life. 

When Terri and I returned to Missouri in the summer of 1972 to begin graduate work at SMSU, my first major tool investment was a heavy-duty electric arc welder for the shop. That machine paid for itself a hundred times over. 

On our farm and across decades of homesteading, I never paid outside welding shops to repair fractured iron. When farm equipment cracked, I prepped the bevels, laid down clean structural beads, and put the machine back into service. 

I designed and fabricated two heavy-duty custom canoe trailers from raw steel tubing for river expeditions. I built a massive, water-filled heavy steel pasture roller to smooth hay ground, and completely salvaged, rebuilt, and welded a dilapidated manure spreader back into prime working condition. 

When Duane Huechteman’s mobile home was annihilated by the 1974 tornado, we hauled out cutting torches, salvaged the twisted chassis, straightened two eighteen-foot steel I-beams, and welded together a heavy-duty tandem-axle farm utility trailer that carried hundreds of tons of hay, building supplies, and scrap over forty years. 

Later, when pouring concrete foundations and basement walls for our passive solar house and home additions, I welded custom steel modular form brackets. 

My crowning artistic piece of ironwork sits at the entrance of our home: a twenty-foot-wide, decorative rolling security gate across the driveway. I engineered the steel frame, joined the ornamental geometric pickets, balanced the roller track, and wired a dedicated 12-volt solar panel and battery system to power the motor and mechanical drive logic.

Learning to burn rod and join steel permanently altered how I saw the material world. To a man with an arc welder, broken machinery is never junk; it is just malleable steel waiting for heat, alignment, and a steady hand.

* **Core Heuristic & Persona Takeaway:**
  * **Tradecraft as Personal Sovereignty:** Mastering high-value physical trades (like structural welding) provides permanent independence from external contractors, turning scrap and salvaged steel into generational utility.
  * **Asymmetric Career Alignment:** Huge financial windfalls (like mining bauxite in Australia) are bad trades if they demand surrendering your personal autonomy, family life, or long-term vocational telos.
  * **First-Principles Asset Rebirth:** Broken steel and twisted disaster wreckage are simply raw building blocks waiting for heat, physics, and creative fabrication.

---

### Story 5.15: The Commercial Widow, Casper's Quonset Hut, and the $12 Bundy — College Living and Rent Routes (c. 1968–1970)

* **Search Triggers / Alternate Titles:** SMS undergraduate living arrangements, MS disabled widow basement apartment, driving for rent, commercial rent collection route Springfield, Caspers chili diner Quonset hut, Glenstone Casper's rent collection, Howard Johnson waitress roommate, Springfield Symphony oboe player, making oboe reeds, twelve dollar Bundy oboe swap shop, SMS instrument repair shop, college bachelor years.
* **Key People:** Commercial property widow, Full-time home nurse, Symphony oboist roommate/student, Swap shop owner, Jim Evans.
* **Visual Artifact / Photo Link:** *(None cataloged)*

When I enrolled at Southwest Missouri State after recovering from the Volkswagen rollover, I didn't commute back and forth from the farm in Republic. As a college student operating on strict frugality, I sought out practical living arrangements that traded service and sweat equity for a roof over my head.

For about a year, I lived in the finished basement apartment of a wealthy Springfield woman who was paralyzed and confined to bed from advanced Multiple Sclerosis. She was the widow of a prominent local dentist who had methodically invested their earnings into prime regional commercial real estate. While she employed a full-time nurse for her medical and personal care, there were long stretches of the day and evening when she was completely alone. 

In exchange for my rent, my duties were straightforward: I helped transfer her from her bed, loaded her into her automobile, and took her for long afternoon drives around the county. 

She was a delight to visit with—sharp as a tack, full of dry wit, and eager to recount colorful stories of the escapades she and her late husband had shared in their younger years. Once a month, our drives became an active seminar in real estate mechanics: we drove the commercial route to collect rents on her holdings. 

She owned the physical real estate for a local ice-skating rink, a Dairy Queen, two busy restaurants, and a gun shop. My absolute favorite stop on the circuit was on South Glenstone at an iconic corrugated-steel Quonset hut: Casper's. Casper Lederer had started out selling fruit and chili from a cart downtown back in 1909, and after World War II, they hauled that surplus military hut onto Glenstone. By the time I was driving the rent route, Casper's son Charlie was running the grill. 

Whenever we pulled up to collect the monthly lease check, the cooks would bring us hot ham-and-cheese sandwiches and bowls of rich pinto bean soup simmered in ham stock. It was my very first hands-on look at the steady mechanics of unencumbered commercial property leases.

Eventually, wanting a more independent routine, I moved out to share an off-campus apartment with an attractive female student. I was working the bellman desk at the Howard Johnson's Motor Lodge on Glenstone, and she took a job as a waitress in the adjoining restaurant. We pooled our paychecks, split the grocery and utility bills straight down the middle, and ran a clean, highly functional household.

She was an exceptionally gifted instrumental music major whose primary focus was the oboe, performing as an active member of the Springfield Symphony. 

The oboe is notoriously temperamental, requiring precision double cane reeds tailored to the player's exact embouchure and breath support. Commercial reeds were expensive and rarely consistent, so I applied my shop patience: I studied the micro-geometry of cane splitting, gouging, and scraping, and became her personal reed maker. Sitting under a desk lamp with razor blades and dial calipers, I shaved and balanced double reeds custom-tuned to her performance specifications, saving her a small fortune across the concert season.

My eye for mechanical salvage paid off in local swap shops. Prowling through a junk store one afternoon, I spotted an oboe in a weathered velvet case sitting on a back shelf. It was an authentic Bundy—a reputable student and professional workhorse brand—and the body, keywork, and pads were in pristine physical shape. 

I asked the proprietor for the price. He shrugged and told me it was marked down to $15 because "it was missing its mouthpiece." The shopkeeper was obviously completely ignorant of woodwind mechanics, unaware that an oboe does not use a clarinet or saxophone mouthpiece, but rather inserts a double reed directly into the top cork receiver. 

I didn't let my excitement show. I hesitated, turned it over in my hands with a skeptical look, and offered him twelve dollars cash. He took it on the spot. 

Given the rock-bottom pawn setting, it was almost certainly a stolen high school instrument from somewhere across the county, but the title was settled. We took the instrument directly to the SMS musical instrument repair class on campus. The instructors and advanced students spent the semester overhauling the key mechanisms, replacing worn pads, and oiling the bore, returning it at the end of the term in flawless, concert-ready playing condition. It immediately became her primary daily performance instrument.

When the Army draft lottery came knocking in the spring of 1970 and my induction orders arrived in August, that collegiate chapter came to an abrupt, clean conclusion. I packed my duffel bag, shipped out to basic training, and arrived at Fort Sill, Oklahoma, unattached and romantically free—setting the board clear for the evening I’d walk into the Caprice Lounge and meet Terri.

* **Core Heuristic & Persona Takeaway:**
  * **Asymmetric Sweat-Equity Housing:** Young independence is achieved by providing reliable service in exchange for overhead. Exchanging companionship and driving for rent provided quiet study space and a front-row seat to commercial real estate management.
  * **Knowledge Arbitrage in Tradecraft:** Specialized technical knowledge (knowing that oboes don't use mouthpieces) turns neglected swap-shop clutter into valuable, high-grade tools at an 80% discount.
  * **The Cross-Disciplinary Maker:** Mechanical patience transfers across domains; the same steady hands and precision measurement used to rebuild carburetors and set point gaps can carve micro-millimeter cane reeds for a symphony woodwind.

---

### Story 5.16: The Lawton Chapel, Two Witnesses, and Fifty-Four Years — Marrying Terri on January 10, 1972 (1972)

* **Search Triggers / Alternate Titles:** January 10 1972 wedding, Lawton Oklahoma chapel wedding, two witnesses small ceremony, Betty Hubbard wedding witness, Ron Hinton Army best man, zero debt wedding, Tennessee road trip best man, Betty Hubbard dementia Cheryl suicide, 60th anniversary vow renewal.
* **Key People:** Terri Evans (wife), Jim Evans, Betty Hubbard (Terri's close friend & witness), Ron Hinton (Army best buddy & best man), Cheryl Hubbard, Brenda Hubbard.
* **Visual Artifact / Photo Link:** *(None cataloged)*

On January 10, 1972, Terri and I were married in Lawton, Oklahoma. 

We didn't take on debt, hire a fleet of limousines, or orchestrate an elaborate, high-stress social production. With my military service winding down, our finances focused strictly on the future, and both of us balancing school and work, the circumstances of the time made a big, planned wedding impossible. 

Instead, we walked into a small Lawton chapel, stood before a local minister, and kept the proceedings grounded in what actually mattered. The law required two official witnesses to sign the registry, and we chose the two people who anchored that chapter of our lives.

Terri chose her close friend, Betty Hubbard. Betty was the loyal companion who had taken Terri in and consoled her on that chaotic evening in 1971 when I was sitting in the post stockade over the commissary belt mix-up and Terri thought I had stood her up. Standing beside me as my best man was Ron Hinton, my closest Army buddy from our Fort Sill battery. 

Betty was a steady, warm presence in Terri's early adult years. Later in life, tragedy struck her home when her nineteen-year-old daughter, Cheryl, died by suicide—a heartbreaking blow that tested the family's endurance. In her final years, Betty battled advanced dementia; her surviving daughter, Brenda, moved Betty to Texas and devotedly cared for her until the end. 

Ron Hinton was the salt of the earth. Raised in a small town west of Nashville, Tennessee, Ron was the kind of steady, dependable friend who never put on airs. After we were both discharged from the service, Terri and I loaded up the car and drove east to Tennessee so I could return the honor, standing as the best man in Ron's own wedding. Over the next fifty years, though separated by hundreds of miles of highway, Ron and I maintained an unbroken line of communication, trading handwritten Christmas cards and updates year after year without fail.

Terri and I occasionally talked about staging an elaborate formal "redo" or a large reception down the road when time and money were plentiful, but life always had more practical projects waiting: framing a solar house, working cattle, teaching school, and seeing the world. The lavish redo never happened, and neither of us ever missed it for a second. 

Starting a marriage with two working hands, a clear conscience, zero consumer debt, and two dependable witnesses turned out to be the finest foundation we could have built. We've weathered fifty-four years of life together on that simple ceremony—and maybe, when we hit our 60th anniversary, we'll finally get around to renewing those vows.

* **Core Heuristic & Persona Takeaway:**
  * **Function Over Social Spectacle:** A marriage is a binding covenant and operational partnership, not a theatrical performance; investing resources into the shared future rather than an extravagant single-day celebration builds lasting financial and emotional stability.
  * **Unbroken Loyalty Across Distance:** True camaraderie does not require daily chatter; a steady annual ledger (like fifty years of Christmas cards with an Army brother) keeps lifelong bonds intact across generations.
  * **Enduring Grounding:** Reject the need for cosmetic "do-overs"; when a commitment is forged with genuine intent and personal integrity, the simplicity of the beginning becomes a badge of honor.

---

# Module 6: The Builder's Creed, Homesteading & Structural Craft

---

### Story 6.1: Building Our Home: First-Principles Architecture, Passive Solar, and the 10,000-Square-Foot Compromise (1975-1976)

* Search Triggers / Alternate Titles: Building solar home 1976, passive solar design, 10000 square foot compromise, 2x6 exterior framing, 18/12 roof pitch, thermodynamics building, building house by hand, fiberglass insulation laryngitis.
* Key People: Terri (wife and building partner).
* Visual Artifact / Photo Link: ![Our Solar Home](https://i.postimg.cc/CLTMmTLY/Our-Solar-Home.jpg) ![House I built 1983 snow storm](https://i.postimg.cc/HW6PwHs0/House-I-built-1983-snow-storm.png) ![Finishing the lights on completed deck](https://i.postimg.cc/SQgt6qN7/Finishing-the-lights-on-completed-deck.jpg)

After five years of marriage, Terri and I decided it was time to build our own home and move out of the cramped, four-room rock house we had lived in since our wedding day. Never mind that we were both teaching full-time while running a thirty-five-head cow-calf beef cattle operation on the side. We were twenty-eight years old and operating with the absolute conviction that there was nothing we could not build, fix, or master ourselves.

We were not hiring a general contractor to manage sub-crews; we planned to do the framing, electrical wiring, thermal insulation, plumbing, siding, roof decking, shingling, and interior trim with our own hands.

We spent the winter of 1975-1976 drafting blueprints, aiming to have the foundation dug and poured in early spring so we could start framing the day school let out for the summer. With high inflation and the 1970s energy crisis, heating and cooling efficiency sat at the center of my calculations.

I dove into architectural thermodynamics and passive solar engineering. The structure needed to optimize surface-area-to-volume ratio to minimize heat transfer. I specified 2x6 exterior framing for thicker insulation cavities, incorporated significant interior thermal mass to store BTUs, and designed an integrated passive solar profile calculating roof overhangs and southern glazing to capture low-angle winter sunlight while completely shading the interior from high summer radiation. The result was a south-facing two-story profile over a full basement, capped with a steep 18/12 roof pitch.

The program called for a kitchen, primary suite, open dining and living rooms, and two full baths on the ground floor; three bedrooms and a shared bath upstairs; and a full drive-in walkout basement for my workshop and mechanical equipment.

I pulled out my drafting board, T-squares, and structural engineering load tables to sketch the layout without artificial square-footage constraints. Every time I presented a concept to Terri, she offered practical feedback: "This room needs to be larger," "We need a doorway here," "That closet isn't big enough," "There's not enough unbroken wall space for our furniture," and so on.

After several rounds of drafting revisions, frustration got the better of me. I told her, "Alright, I am going to draw a master set that includes literally every single dimension, feature, and space you want without cutting a corner."

When I calculated the footprint, it totaled nearly 10,000 square feet--a massive estate far beyond our budget to finance or build by hand. We had a good laugh, faced reality, and agreed on a realistic compromise: a tight, highly functional 2,500 square feet across the top two living floors, with another 1,200 square feet of unfinished drive-in basement for later development. I drew the optimized blueprints, and we were officially ready to break ground.

Terri worked right alongside me through every phase of construction, matching the physical labor stride for stride. But when we hit the insulation stage, packing all that dense fiberglass batt into the deep 2x6 cavities took a heavy toll. Breathing in the airborne dust and fibers gave her a severe, acute case of laryngitis that wiped out her voice completely. Not a whisper or a squeak would come out.

The real challenge was not just on the job site--it was at her day job. Terri was a full-time English teacher, and standing in front of a classroom full of students all day without a functioning voice was an absolute trial. She had to run her entire class by writing notes: scribbling instructions on notepads, writing assignments across the chalkboard, and communicating silently. Through it all, she kept her sense of humor, eventually scrawling a note to me teasing that she suspected I was secretly enjoying the silence around the house.

* Core Heuristic & Persona Takeaway:
  * Applied Thermodynamics & Systems Design: Apply first-principles physics (passive solar gain, thermal mass, insulation cavities) to solve structural efficiency challenges.
  * Pragmatic Compromise: Balance ambitious design requirements with cold financial realities, engineering an optimized solution without taking on destructive leverage.

---

### Story 6.2: The Builder's Creed -- First-Principles Engineering, Self-Reliance, and the 8080 Microprocessor (c. 1950s-Present)

* Search Triggers / Alternate Titles: Builder philosophy, DIY projects, 1975 8080 microprocessor build, 1976 solar home build, solar array installation, certified welder, furniture craftsman, learning surgical procedures, practical engineering skills.
* Key People: Alan McMurtry (lifelong childhood friend), Terri (wife and construction partner), Dad (mechanical mentor).
* Visual Artifact / Photo Link: ![Grandma's Butterscotch](https://i.postimg.cc/TYhgZvjw/Grandma-s-Butterscotch.jpg) ![Back Yard shed and deck](https://i.postimg.cc/jqchjZ97/Back-Yard-shed-and-deck.jpg) ![Book Cabinet](https://i.postimg.cc/MTbmVDGR/Book-Cabinete.jpg) ![China Cabinet](https://i.postimg.cc/BbScgK8b/China-Cabinete.jpg) ![Dressing Mirror](https://i.postimg.cc/T1dq9Dyc/Dressing-Mirror.jpg) ![LED Christmas Display](https://i.postimg.cc/C5cC6xD2/LED-Christmas-Display.jpg) ![Night Stand](https://i.postimg.cc/yx1XPZJg/Night-Stand.jpg) ![PV Array](https://i.postimg.cc/6qMV1pRk/PV-Array.jpg) ![Rolling Powered gate](https://i.postimg.cc/QC8kbTB5/Rolling-Powered-gate.jpg) ![Walk-in Shower](https://i.postimg.cc/bryHTtGF/Walk-in-Shower.jpg)

From the earliest days I can remember on the farm and in the shop, I was given the tools and the freedom to build, troubleshoot, and fix anything that crossed my path. That environment forged an instinct that has driven my entire life: if a machine, structure, or system is broken, you do not wait for permission or pay someone else to solve it--you learn how it works and you build the solution yourself.

My foundational mechanical education started shoulder-to-shoulder with my dad. I learned internal combustion principles by rebuilding carburetors, setting timing, and dialing in spark plugs until engines purred. I learned how to true bicycle wheels by adjusting spoke tension, repairing derailleurs, and repairing flat tires across farm equipment.

Out in the creek beds with my best friend Alan, our play was applied physics: we dammed running streams, rigged paddle wheels to drive rotating fan blades, built rubber-band-powered boats, engineered match-head rockets, and wound string-pull helicopters. We mastered paper aerodynamics and learned practical field skills--how to free a stuck vehicle, pilot a truck through deep Ozarks snow, balance a wagon load, stack hay bales tight to the rafters, and strategically place black snakes in the grain bins to protect seed from mice.

Whenever I face an unknown domain, my reflex is deep research. When I had to undergo major surgical procedures--an inguinal hernia repair, a cholecystectomy, and a prostatectomy--I tracked down clinical surgical videos and studied the exact operative mechanics before ever rolling into the operating theater. Understanding the physical mechanics of a system brings clarity, resilience, and calm.

Before finishing school, I had self-taught small engine mechanics, major appliance repair, structural framing, metal fabrication, and fine woodworking. The moment I was discharged from the Army in the early 1970s, I studied the technical standards and passed the exams to earn my certified welder credentials. I went on to complete a Bachelor of Science in Mathematics, a Master of Arts in Mathematics, and a degree in Computer Science. In 1975, well before personal computers were sold commercially in retail stores, I sourced an Intel 8080 microprocessor, wired the bus architecture, and built my own functioning computer from bare components.

In 1976, I designed and built our family's two-story home from the ground up, utilizing only hourly labor and Terri's steady assistance. That house was a living laboratory of passive solar thermal design, integrated active solar heating, and solar thermal water circulation. In 2010, I worked with an electrician friend to install a 13.5 kW grid-tied photovoltaic array. Later, I engineered and installed an entire 8.5 kW rooftop solar array at our residence completely solo--navigating utility interconnect approvals, rewiring the main electrical breaker panel, and fabricating a custom battery-powered hoist to lift the heavy solar panels onto the roof safely.

Walking through our home today, fifteen major pieces of fine hardwood furniture are pieces I personally designed, joined, and hand-finished in the shop.

The Builder's Practical Skill Registry:
- Structural & Civil: Timber framing, concrete finishing, roofing, vinyl siding, drywall hanging and glass-smooth finishing, tree felling.
- Electrical & Solar: Residential circuit design and rewiring, breaker panel replacements, photovoltaic array engineering and grid-tied installation.
- Mechanical & Fabrication: Certified welding, structural metal fabrication, automotive timing and carburetor rebuilds, appliance repair, plumbing rough-in and finish work.
- Hardware & Software: Microprocessor circuit assembly (Intel 8080), computer application coding, algorithmic logic.
- Craft & Domestic Arts: Fine furniture design and joinery, homestead butchering, baking homemade butterscotch pies.
- Biological & Physical Mastery: Heavy functional bodybuilding, marathon conditioning, deep mobility work, yoga postures, and mindfulness practice.

While I have worn many titles over fifty-four years of marriage--soldier, public school mathematics teacher, cross-country coach, software developer, distance runner, and political candidate--none of them fully capture my core nature. At my bedrock, I am a Builder.

* Core Heuristic & Persona Takeaway:
  * First-Principles Competence: Never accept learned helplessness; break complex mechanical, structural, or biological problems down to their underlying physics and master the execution.
  * The Builder Identity: True sovereignty comes from the quiet confidence that whatever needs designing, assembling, wiring, repairing, or finishing, you possess the intellect and physical capability to build it yourself.

---

### Story 6.3: The Geometric Progression -- Raw Sewage, Goodwill Heaps, and Micro-Plastic Soot (1984-1988)

* Search Triggers / Alternate Titles: Real estate investing, rental property math, geometric progression debt compounding, Ernie SPS art teacher 500 units, crawlspace duck-walk on five-gallon buckets, cracked sewer line under duplex, Grace St unwashed clothes landfill, melted stereo speaker fire, micro-plastic soot Kilz remediation, Springfield rentals (Grace, Garfield, Park, Lombard).
* Key People: Everett & Sharon Isaacs, Ernie (SPS art teacher and 500-unit mentor), Anna Marie (Ernie's realtor wife), Teresa "Terri" Evans.
* Visual Artifact / Photo Link: ![Back Yard shed and deck](https://i.postimg.cc/jqchjZ97/Back-Yard-shed-and-deck.jpg)

Back in college, one of my mathematics professors laid out an elegant theory of real estate: it was an exponential geometric progression. You bought a starter property with a modest loan, let the tenant pay the note with a small margin left over, acquired a second, and applied the combined cash flows to retire the debt early. As each property cleared, its unencumbered rent accelerated the payout on the next acquisition like a compounding snowball.

Years later, while teaching at Study Junior High, that classroom theory walked straight into the faculty lounge. My running companion and fellow teacher Everett Isaacs and his wife Sharon became close friends with Terri and me. Sharon's brother, Ernie, was an art teacher in Springfield Public Schools, and his wife Anna Marie had earned her real estate license to locate underpriced residential properties. Ernie had put the professor's math into practice: by the time I took serious notice, his rental cash flow was paying off an entire additional house every six months. I grew up diagnosing machinery on a dairy farm and building my own home, so the mechanical trades did not intimidate me. If Ernie could swing a hammer and teach art, I could teach calculus and turn a pipe wrench.

My real-world apprenticeship arrived on a Saturday night when Ernie called asking if I was free Sunday morning. He had an absolute nightmare of a repair and needed another set of hands. One of his duplexes had suffered a structural disaster: the primary cast-iron sewer line had cracked under the subfloor, dumping approximately eight inches of standing raw sewage directly into the enclosed crawlspace.

Early Sunday, we dug an exterior trench beneath the concrete foundation right where the line breached, allowing the bulk of the black water to drain into the yard. We mounted an electric shop fan into a foundation vent to suck the toxic fumes and suffocating stench out into the open air. Then we grabbed a pair of plastic five-gallon buckets to use as rolling stools, dropped into the damp, putrid crawlspace, and spent hours duck-walking across the sludge, unbolting rotted cast iron and gluing in clean white PVC.

During one of our breathers--perched side-by-side on five-gallon buckets in that dark, rank subterranean trench--we talked business. Ernie shook his head and noted that his monthly lumberyard bill now vastly exceeded his teacher's paycheck. He was seriously considering resigning from SPS to roll his entire retirement account into buying more rentals. I tried to talk him down, reminding him of pensions and job security.

Ernie looked at me through the crawlspace murk, shrugged, and dropped a line that stuck with me for decades: "You know, I'd rather be down here doing this than standing in front of a classroom trying to track ungrateful kids."

He put in his notice, resigned from teaching, and went all in. At the peak of his career, Ernie owned over 500 rental units.

Between 1984 and 1987, I built my own portfolio in Springfield, buying Grace Street, Garfield, Park, and Lombard. Ernie taught me how to turn over a vacant house in seventy-two hours and manage tenant friction before it turned litigious. Managing unruly junior high students provided the behavioral psychology, and farm maintenance provided the tradecraft. 

Yet the domestic habits of the tenants provided an education of their own. 

On Grace Street, I leased to a pleasant woman who managed a local Hardee's, living there with her teenage daughter and a dog. She paid her rent reliably on the first of every month, but her household operations were staggering. Over several years, I went in to fix light switches, patch dripping faucets, replace burned-out range burners, and install a new water heater. Each time, I was appalled by the interior conditions and quietly dreaded the cleanup when she eventually vacated.

She maintained a unique personal philosophy: she simply did not believe in washing clothes. Whenever the daughter needed clean outfits, they drove to Goodwill, bought armloads of cheap garments, and threw them on the floor when worn. When she finally moved out, the bedroom floor had completely vanished under an undulating landfill. Shoveling through that excavation site with a grain scoop, I uncovered dirty clothes, toys, dried makeup, stuffed animals, fossilized slices of pizza, and dog poop. I dragged out over fifty 50-gallon contractor yard-bags packed with grimy discards, bleached the subfloors, put on fresh paint, and sold the property--doubling my investment.

Shortly after, dispatch from the Springfield fire department called me late at night. The fire was out, but the cause was classic: the renter had balanced a burning wax candle on top of a plastic stereo speaker, gotten high, and passed out on the couch. He woke up to a burning speaker shooting flames into a window curtain, with the interior suffocated by pitch-black smoke.

The open flames were contained quickly, but the petrochemical smoke was devastating. The vaporized plastic housing had combined with the grease to generate billions of airborne micro-plastic soot droplets that bonded like epoxy to every surface in the house. Cabinets, appliances, trim, doors, and ceilings were coated in an abrasive, sticky film that resisted every solvent. I had to collect the insurance payout, hire professional remediation crews to coat every square inch in heavy oil-based Kilz, refinish the surfaces, and replace the appliances. 

Once the smoke cleared, I planted a "For Sale" sign in the yard. A clear rule had emerged: buy neglected starter homes, leverage sweat equity to renovate, absorb the lessons of tenant psychology, and capitalize the equity into cleaner pastures.

* Core Heuristic & Persona Takeaway:
  * The Geometric Leverage Engine: Compounding rental cash flows to pay off principal balances accelerates capital far faster than relying on a linear W-2 salary.
  * The Sweat-Equity Moat: If an investor cannot pick up tools and handle plumbing or electrical failures personally, contractor invoices will consume operating margins.
  * Know When to Exit: When a property experiences a structural or petrochemical disaster, execute thorough repairs, capture the market equity, and reinvest the proceeds into better-controlled inventory.

---

### Story 6.4: Raising Walnut Street -- Ten Hydraulic Jacks and the Attic Lord (1988-1992)

* Search Triggers / Alternate Titles: Republic MO rentals, Walnut Street termite house, 10 hydraulic bottle jacks house leveling, Jimmy Nichols math teacher carpentry, Nicholas dropping tools in foundation blocks, Nicholas gets blasted by plumbing waterline test, "the lord lives up there", Bill the over-the-road trucker tenant, "next Friday" finish date, 7-year consolidated commercial loan.
* Key People: Jimmy Nichols (high school math teacher and carpentry partner), Nicholas and his mother (tenants on Walnut), Bill (trucker tenant), Teresa "Terri" Evans.
* Visual Artifact / Photo Link: ![House I built 1983 snow storm](https://i.postimg.cc/HW6PwHs0/House-I-built-1983-snow-storm.png)

Tired of late-night emergency drives into Springfield, I liquidated my four city properties and redeployed the capital back into my hometown of Republic, rolling multiple residential acquisitions into a single, self-liquidating seven-year commercial mortgage.

The most severe structural puzzle was a tiny, neglected two-room house on Walnut Street. It sat on a great residential lot, and I bought it outright for just $7,500. The bargain basement price stemmed from its modest 768-square-foot footprint and severe termite damage. Subterranean termites had hollowed out the perimeter rim joists and stud bases so completely that the structure had collapsed inward: the floor in the northwest corner was a full seven inches lower than the southeast corner. Walking across the living room felt like walking the deck of a listing vessel.

Because the perimeter concrete masonry foundation was intact, I decided to level it myself. I bought a bunk of pressure-treated framing lumber, purchased ten 12-ton hydraulic bottle jacks, and recruited a fellow high school math teacher, Jimmy Nichols, with the promise of practical physics and cold beer.

Jimmy and I cut structural pockets through the floor framing directly above the masonry walls, set hardwood cribbing, and placed all ten hydraulic bottle jacks under the main spans. We set up transit line levels and began pumping the handles, hoisting the building a quarter-inch at a time. Once the building hung level in mid-air, we cut away the termite-riddled framing, spliced in new treated sill plates and structural rim joists, and eased the house back down onto its foundation.

With the original two rooms level, I broke ground off the back, framing a two-bedroom, two-bathroom addition that doubled the home's layout to 1,500 square feet.

While framing was underway, I rented the unrenovated front section for $50 a month to a single mother and her young son, Nicholas, with the written agreement that as soon as the back addition was dried-in, they would move into the new space for $300 a month. 

That arrangement produced instant local color. Shortly after work began, my hand tools began vanishing. After spending an hour searching for an expensive socket set, I caught young Nicholas gleefully dropping wrenches, screwdrivers, and tape measures straight down into the hollow cores of the concrete block foundation. I solved that game immediately by bolting down the treated wooden sill plates.

A few days later, after tying the rafters together, I fired up a gas chainsaw, cut an opening through the exterior wall to connect the two attics, installed a 200-amp breaker panel, and climbed into the ceiling rafters to run the Romex wiring. 

Around noon, Terri drove over with a hot lunch. Finding the lower rooms empty, she spotted Nicholas in the hallway and asked if he had seen Jim. Nicholas stopped, pointed a wide-eyed finger directly up at the ceiling opening, and whispered: 

"The lord lives up there."

A couple of evenings later, I put in a late shift soldering all the interior copper water lines. I installed shutoff valves at every fixture stub, turned the main meter wide open to check for leaks, and seeing no drops, decided to leave the lines pressurized overnight. 

The next morning, I walked into the south bathroom to find the subfloor and ceiling drenched directly beneath an uncapped toilet supply line. I knew immediately what had transpired. A few minutes later, Nicholas wandered into the room, looking sheepish and damp.

I looked down at him: "Did it get you?"

He nodded quietly: "Uh huh."

That was the entirety of the investigation; he never touched a plumbing valve again.

When the addition was finished, Nicholas and his mother moved elsewhere, freeing me to gut and remodel the original front rooms into a modern living room and kitchen, turning the original bath into a utility room. 

By late May, the property was ready to rent, save for minor punch-list items: touch-up paint, some trim work, and finish cabinet hardware. While I was packing my tools, an over-the-road trucker named Bill pulled into the drive and asked how soon it would be ready. 

I looked at the wet trim and said, "Next Friday."

Bill pulled out a roll of bills, paid the security deposit and first month's rent in cash, took the keys, unloaded a duffel bag, and drove off in his semi-truck. Bill was an ideal tenant: he was over the road three weeks out of the month, stayed maybe five days every thirty, and always paid ahead of schedule in cash. If he was out of state when rent came due, he mailed a postal money order from whatever truck stop he was idling in.

A couple of years later, Bill stopped by my shop, laughed, and said: 

"You know, Jim, you never actually specified which Friday you were gonna finish."

He was right. Those tiny punch-list chores--the paint touch-ups and cabinet adjustments--remained unfinished. But I had installed a heavy-duty backyard fence for his dog, so by Ozarks accounting, we were square. When I eventually sold that $7,500 termite wreck, I cleared $65,000 in cash without ever taking a dime of bank debt on the remodel.

* Core Heuristic & Persona Takeaway:
  * First-Principles Structural Salvage: Never walk away from a sagging structure if the foundation is sound; ten hydraulic jacks, treated timber, and basic physics can turn an unsellable property into prime equity.
  * Ozark Worksite Humor: Managing live-in tenants during renovations requires thick skin, patience for curious children, and an appreciation for the lighter moments of construction.
  * The "Next Friday" Truce: Functional comfort and mutual accommodation (like building a dog fence) matter far more to good tenants than cosmetic perfection.

---

### Story 6.5: The Grader-Blade Fortress -- Curtis on the Roof, The 34-Year Handshake, and the Mockingbird Portfolio (1992-Present)

* Search Triggers / Alternate Titles: Rosewood metal roof accident, Curtis sitting under the oak tree saying sue yourself, shinbone cut IV antibiotics, Republic duplex love triangle tragedy, grader blade reinforced concrete house, Hines Street, Randy and Ronda Branson 34-year tenants, trust deed inheritance gift, Shari Posey divorce bailout, Chickasha bungalow, Lawton Mockingbird Lane auction houses, Lawton Rentals property management.
* Key People: Curtis (Morningside tenant), Randy, Ronda, and Adam Branson (34-year tenants on Hines St), Shari Posey (sister-in-law), Teresa "Terri" Evans.
* Visual Artifact / Photo Link: ![Our house showing deck landscaping and drive in garage](https://i.postimg.cc/c41QyhQV/Our-house-showing-deck-landscapin-and-drive-in-garage.jpg)

Real estate investing provides financial independence, but it can also expose an owner to sharp edges--both physical and human.

The physical lesson arrived on Rosewood Street, a 1960s house with a low-pitched roof that began to leak. I ordered corrugated standing-seam metal panels to replace the rotting shingles, stacked the sheets straddling the roof ridge, and got to work. 

My tenant from around the corner on Morningside, Curtis, saw me working and ambled over to "help." Curtis's help consisted entirely of conversation. While I sweated in the midday heat driving screws, Curtis found a patch of shade under an oak tree, sat down, and chattered away.

By mid-afternoon, dark thunderstorm clouds were building in the southwest. Racing to seal the roof deck before the deluge hit, I lost situational awareness between the storm panic and Curtis's unrelenting chatter. I pivoted sharply and walked directly into the razor-sharp edge of the last metal sheet.

The steel gouged straight across my shin. I dropped onto the deck, ripped off my sweaty T-shirt, tore the fabric into strips with a pocketknife, and wrapped a tight tourniquet around my leg. With purple clouds looming, I drove home the final sheet and secured the ridge cap.

From his comfortable seat under the oak tree, Curtis offered his assessment: "You know, Jim, you oughta sue."

I looked down through the sweat and blood: "Sue who, Curtis? I'm the owner."

At the emergency clinic, the doctor discovered that the steel had cut clean through the periosteum into the tibia bone itself. They scrubbed the wound, stitched me up, and admitted me for intravenous antibiotic therapy. The medical bills totaled more than the pallet of roofing metal--one DIY project that saved no money at all.

The human friction of multi-family property proved even more sobering. I owned two adjoining duplexes by the elementary school where tenants shared common yards. The close proximity bred constant domestic squabbles, requiring me to act as peace officer over parked cars, loud music, and trash cans. 

The friction turned tragic when police called late one night: a tenant had discovered his live-in girlfriend was having an affair with the husband in the matching duplex unit. Distraught, the young man went into the shared backyard and hanged himself from a high oak limb. Seeing the flashing emergency beacons on my lot line, I realized my commercial debt across Republic was fully paid off. I was debt-free, and these duplexes no longer fit my life; I sold them immediately.

Yet the very next acquisition balanced the ledger. In 1992, I found a structural oddity on Hines Street built by a municipal heavy equipment operator. He had poured the structure using solid concrete block and tied the floor spans together with concrete beams reinforced with discarded city road-grader cutting blades. The HVAC was shot and the wiring was archaic, but the grader-blade frame was practically bombproof.

While I was gutting the interior, a young man named Randy Branson stopped by to ask if I planned to rent it. I pointed out the tangled wiring and missing ductwork. Randy smiled and revealed he was an electrician. We struck a deal on a handshake: if he pulled new Romex to code and helped me install the HVAC ducting, I would waive his security deposit and give him the first month free.

Randy and I put in hundreds of hours wiring that grader-blade fortress, building a lasting friendship. When the work wrapped, Randy told me that once he, his wife Ronda, and their boy Adam moved in, they would never leave. 

That handshake occurred 34 years ago. The Bransons still live in that home today. 

They have never missed a rent payment, and they maintain the mechanics as if they owned it. Because of their loyalty, I never raised their rent to match market inflation; they pay roughly half the market rate, and their payments have covered my initial purchase price three times over. When Terri and I drew up our estate trust, we knew my niece had no interest in managing Republic rentals. We deeded the Hines Street property directly to Randy and Ronda Branson as a permanent, debt-free gift upon our passing.

The final phase of my real estate journey served as a family safety net. In 2002, Terri's sister Shari faced a difficult divorce in Oklahoma, burdened by an underwater mobile home. We stepped in, driving to Chickasha to buy a clean two-bedroom bungalow for cash so she had a secure roof over her head. 

When the local Delta Faucet plant closed in 2006, Shari found work at a car dealership in Lawton, where her son, grandchildren, and extended family lived. To eliminate her commute, we sold the Chickasha home at cost and bought a starter house for cash in Lawton. While visiting, I noticed an auction banner in the front yard of the adjacent property; dialing in via our broker, I won the parcel for half of what we paid for Shari's home.

In 2017, wanting to provide Shari with lasting comfort, we bought a modern, three-bedroom brick home in an established Lawton subdivision. We retained the two adjacent Mockingbird Lane houses, turning them over to Lawton Rentals to oversee the tenants, coordinate contractors, and deposit the monthly proceeds.

From sewage crawlspaces to grader-blade beams, rental properties demanded real physical resilience. But measured by equity compounded, life lessons learned, and the ability to shelter family and reward loyal friends, it was one of the most profitable investments of a lifetime.

* Core Heuristic & Persona Takeaway:
  * Relational Equity Trumps Maximum Yield: A great tenant who protects the property (like the Bransons) is worth far more than theoretical market returns; true financial freedom allows you to give the asset away.
  * Job Site Focus Is Life Safety: Never allow chatty spectators under oak trees to break your concentration when working with sharp metal under a storm; situational awareness protects against emergency room visits.
  * Real Estate as a Family Shield: The true value of accumulated equity is the ability to step in with cash to stabilize family members during times of crisis.

---

### Story 6.6: Housebuilding Trials -- The Quitting Plumber, Smiley's 150-PSI Copper, and the 13-Year Curio (1976-1990s)

* Search Triggers / Alternate Titles: House plumbing story, plumber quit note on wall, Smiley Teide HVAC friend, 150 psi copper sweat weld, soap bubble leak test, Terri fiberglass insulation bronchitis, lost voice two weeks, 13 year curio cabinet, painted white wood curing.
* Key People: Terri (wife, co-builder), Smiley Teide (retired HVAC neighbor/mentor).
* Visual Artifact / Photo Link: ![China Cabinet](https://i.postimg.cc/BbScgK8b/China-Cabinete.jpg)

When Terri and I were building our passive solar home in the mid-1970s, we originally planned to contract out a few specialized trades. Plumbing was one of them. We hired a local plumber, but when we returned home from teaching school after his first full day on site, I was appalled at how little pipe he had actually laid. That evening, I picked up the tools and spent three hours roughing in lines myself, accomplishing more in one evening than he had all day. I naively thought he would appreciate the head start; instead, when we arrived the next afternoon, we found a blunt note scribbled on the wall studs: "I quit. I can't have someone messing with my work."

So, like everything else on that job site, I resolved to do all the plumbing myself.

Fortunately, a close neighbor and retired HVAC mechanic named Smiley Teide dropped by frequently to inspect our progress. Smiley saw me wrestling with the drain system and made an offer: if I would cut and dry-fit every rigid copper water line with the fittings in place, he would come over and sweat-solder the whole grid. Smiley was an uncompromising perfectionist who treated residential domestic water lines like a high-pressure commercial refrigeration unit. After soldering every joint, he capped the lines, pressurized the entire house system to 150 PSI of air, painted every soldered fitting with liquid soap, and watched for micro-bubbles. We found two tiny pinhole leaks, resoldered them, and left the system locked under pressure for twenty-four hours. In the forty-nine years since Smiley pressurized that copper, our house has never sprung a single water leak.

Rushing to enclose the framing before drywall crews arrived brought its own misery. While I pulled electrical circuits, Terri spent two grueling days installing thick fiberglass batts to ensure optimal acoustic and thermal insulation. The airborne glass fibers triggered severe acute bronchitis, causing Terri--an English teacher who loves lively conversation--to lose her voice completely for two full weeks. She still jokes that I secretly enjoyed the quiet, though the reality was an avalanche of written notes flying back and forth across the dinner table.

My woodworking projects on the house usually moved faster, with one notable exception: the curio cabinet I promised Terri. Deciding to build it out of inexpensive white wood and paint it, the soft wood refused to cooperate. The primer balled up under the sandpaper and after six frustrating coats without achieving the glass-smooth base I wanted, I shoved the piece into storage to "let the paint cure for six months." Six months became twelve years. Eventually, after buying a high-pressure air sprayer for another build, I dusted off the old cabinet, discovered the primer had finally fully cured, sanded it to glass, sprayed on an immaculate enamel finish, and hung the glass and brass hardware. Thirteen years after making the first cut, Terri finally received her birthday present. She now tells everyone: "Jim always finishes what he starts--provided you can wait long enough."

* Core Heuristic & Persona Takeaway:
  * Smiley's Pressure Standard: When installing infrastructure hidden inside walls, test it far beyond operational limits (150 PSI air and soap bubbles) before closing it up. The cost of precision upfront prevents catastrophic drywall damage decades later.
  * Relentless Follow-Through: A stalled project is not an abandoned project; patience, changing tools, or waiting for materials to cure eventually yields the desired standard.

---

### Story 6.7: The Blizzards of '77-'79 -- 14-Inch Pond Ice, Wood Stoves, and the -17 Degrees F Calf (1977-1979)

* Search Triggers / Alternate Titles: Blizzards of 1977 1978 1979, polar vortex blocked jet stream, 14 inch pond ice, chopping ice for beef cattle, wood stove heating, 1963 pickup jump start, minus 17 calf, Southwest Missouri winter farming.
* Key People: Terri (wife & partner).
* Visual Artifact / Photo Link: ![House I built 1983 snow storm](https://i.postimg.cc/HW6PwHs0/House-I-built-1983-snow-storm.png)

From late 1976 through the winter of 1979, an anomalous, highly amplified jet stream pattern locked Arctic polar air deep across the American Midwest, unleashing an unrelenting series of historic blizzards and sub-zero cold snaps.

For Terri and me, those winters were a brutal trial of endurance. We were living inside our unfinished new house while running a thirty-five-head cow-calf beef cattle operation. Our sole source of interior heat was a single cast-iron wood stove standing in the center of our unfinished ground floor. Teaching high school all day was just our preliminary shift; the real labor began when the final school bell rang, hauling chainsaws into the woods to fell trees and split cords of seasoned firewood just to survive the freezing nights.

Out in the pastures, the cattle relied entirely on us. Farm ponds froze under fourteen solid inches of concrete-hard ice. Every morning and evening, bundled against sub-zero wind chills, I took a heavy axe and spent hours chopping water holes so the herd would not dehydrate. Our 1963 heavy work truck had to be jump-started nearly every morning, its engine coughing to life so we could plow through mountain drifts into the fields to drop square bales. Terri, shivering by the wood stove and exhausted from the relentless frozen mud, was more than ready to pack her bags and head back south to Oklahoma.

The turning point came on a bitter morning in February 1979. The mercury dropped to a bone-cracking 17 degrees below zero. Expecting the worst, I crunched through the snowdrifts to check the herd.

Standing in the shelter of the timber, steam rising off its back, was a newborn calf--dry, healthy, upright, and nursing vigorously. If that fragile little creature could survive 17 below zero on a bed of Missouri snow and stand on its own four feet, we could endure whatever this climate threw at us. It was the quiet sign we needed that the worst was behind us, and spring was on its way.

* Core Heuristic & Persona Takeaway:
  * Stewardship Demands Exposure: When you take responsibility for livestock, family, or infrastructure, you do not get to stay inside where it is warm; you break the ice and turn the ignition regardless of the thermometer.
  * Biological Tenacity as Hope: Life possesses an astonishing capacity to endure extreme hardship; when pressure is at its maximum (-17 degrees F), look for small signs of resilience and hold the line.

---

### Story 6.8: The 40-Year Quest for Net-Zero -- Rock Beds, Solar Arrays, and the Quail Creek Transition (1976-2020)

* Search Triggers / Alternate Titles: Photovoltaic systems, DIY solar array, hot air solar collector, rock pile thermal storage mold dust, net-zero home Missouri, 8.4 kW ground array, 6.5 kW roof solar, Quail Creek move, single-story accessibility, energy independence.
* Key People: Terri (wife & partner).
* Visual Artifact / Photo Link: ![PV Array](https://i.postimg.cc/6qMV1pRk/PV-Array.jpg)

When Terri and I designed our passive solar home during the 1970s energy crisis, my goal was complete energy independence. We moved in during the fall of 1977 and put an active hot-air solar roof and an internal rock-pile thermal storage bed into service during the brutal blizzards of 1977-1979. While the physics worked, the biology failed us: after three years of operation, the continuous air circulation through subterranean rock beds loaded the house with fine dust and mold spores, causing chronic upper-respiratory illness. In 1980, we pulled the plug on the system for our health, hauled out tons of rock to convert the space into my workshop, and installed a conventional heat pump.

The dream of true self-sufficiency never died; the technology just needed thirty years to catch up. By 2010, the economics of photovoltaic silicon had dropped to the point where DIY solar was practical. I purchased 140 one-meter-square, 60-watt panels, engineered and fabricated a ground-mount racking structure south of the house, and wired the 8.4 kW grid-tied array myself. Controlling domestic electrical loads kept our monthly utility bill pinned to the $13 meter minimum nine months out of the year. In 2015, I added a 6.5 kW array on the steep winter-optimized roof pitch. Pumping our own well water with zero natural gas or municipal sewer bills, we became one of the few verified net-zero homes in Missouri--fulfilling the 1975 energy dream forty years later.

Yet engineering homes for youth does not account for aging bodies. Reaching our three-story home required climbing fifteen steep exterior steps from the drive, which became an insurmountable barrier as Terri faced bilateral knee replacements and visiting elderly friends struggled to reach our door. Maintaining three landscaped park-like acres, a white vinyl rail fence, and an 80-acre perimeter shifted from a labor of love into an exhausting grind. In 2018, we made the pragmatic decision to adapt: we sold the rural homestead and bought a level, 2,100-square-foot single-story home in Springfield's Quail Creek neighborhood. By the summer of 2020, I was up on the new roof, wiring another custom photovoltaic array to bring energy independence to our final harbor.

* Core Heuristic & Persona Takeaway:
  * Empirical Pragmatism Over Sunk Costs: When an engineered system produces harmful side effects (like mold and dust from thermal rock beds), abandon it immediately; do not let pride in your design ruin your physical health.
  * Adaptive Structural Transitions: Housing must serve human physiology, not ego. Recognize when multi-story acreage becomes an asymmetric physical liability and transition proactively to single-level accessibility before a crisis forces the move.

---

### Story 6.9: The Civil Ledger — Why Furniture Is a Piece of Cake and Real Labor Is Measured in Tons (c. 1970s–2020s)

* Search Triggers / Alternate Titles: Hardest builds, furniture is a piece of cake, concrete circle drive Republic, 80 acre fence rows, mile of barbed wire, 7500 square foot pole barn, trenching 13kW solar, Quail Creek roof array, true physical labor, civil engineering homestead.
* Key People: Jim Evans, Terri Evans (partner & co-builder), Gene Evans (mechanical mentor).
* Visual Artifact / Photo Link: ![PV Array](https://i.postimg.cc/6qMV1pRk/PV-Array.jpg) ![Our Solar Home](https://i.postimg.cc/CLTMmTLY/Our-Solar-Home.jpg)

Whenever visitors walk through our home and admire the hand-joined hardwood furniture—the cherry nightstands, the oak china cabinet, the dressing mirrors, or the leaded glass displays—they invariably comment on how grueling that work must have been. 

I always have to smile. Compared to the real civil engineering and structural builds we tackled across fifty years, woodworking, building trailers, framing decks, laying retaining walls, and erecting gazebos were an absolute piece of cake. 

Cabinetry is an indoor exercise in patience, sharp layout tools, and compound miter settings. If you miscalculate an angle or split a board, you toss it in the kindling bin, grab another length of dry lumber, and carry on. 

Real labor—the kind that permanently alters your musculoskeletal frame, hardens calluses, and tests your mental grit—is measured in cubic yards, hydraulic pressure, and physical tons of material fighting back against you in the elements. 

The true heavy ledger was written in structural dirt, concrete, and steel:

1. **The Multi-Year Concrete Circle Drive:**  
Circling our home in Republic with a continuous, 10-foot-wide concrete drive was one of the most grueling long-term campaigns I ever undertook. It wasn't just pouring mud; it was excavation, shooting grades with a line level, hauling base rock, bending long curved radius forms to keep the arc true, and calculating exact cubic yardage so the ready-mix trucks didn't leave you short or dump surplus. Once that chute dropped, you were on an unforgiving chemical clock—screeding hundreds of feet of heavy aggregate, bull-floating, cutting expansion joints, and dragging the broom finish before the July sun baked it into unworkable stone.

2. **The 80-Acre Perimeter Fence:**  
Clearing overgrown tree fencerows along three full property boundaries of our 80-acre farm in Republic meant months of wrestling chainsaws, clearing dense Osage orange and locust thorns, hauling brush, and burning piles until your eyes stung. Then came the manual labor: digging postholes into fractured limestone, tamping corner H-braces solid, unrolling spools, and stretching a full mile of four-point barbed wire tight enough to sing when plucked with a hammer.

3. **The 7,500-Square-Foot, 16-Foot-Tall Pole Barn:**  
Building an agricultural structure that spans 7,500 square feet with 16-foot eaves is industrial-scale construction. It demanded setting massive structural poles plumb in deep piers, hoisting heavy engineered roof trusses high into the air with rigging, and walking roof purlins in the wind to screw down thousands of square feet of ribbed steel panels before weather blew in.

4. **The 3,500-Square-Foot Passive Solar Home:**  
Taking a blank drafting board in the winter of 1975 and turning it into a three-story, four-bedroom, three-bath passive solar structure with a full walkout basement was a trial of endurance. Hoisting heavy 2x6 rafters onto a punishing 18/12 roof pitch, hanging hundreds of sheets of dense drywall, pulling electrical home-runs, and packing wall cavities with fiberglass insulation until our lungs burned required two straight years of unyielding physical momentum alongside full-time teaching jobs.

5. **Lifting and Doubling Walnut Street:**  
Placing ten 12-ton hydraulic bottle jacks under a listing, termite-gutted $7,500 house, pumping them in synchronization to lift a sagging building seven inches true in mid-air, and then hand-framing a two-bedroom, two-bath addition off the back doubled the footprint through pure sweat equity.

6. **The Solar Arrays and Trenching:**  
Fabricating the structural steel ground mounts for a 13.5 kW photovoltaic array in 2010 meant digging deep concrete piers, mounting 140 individual glass panels, and hand-digging a deep subterranean trench through packed hardpan clay and rock back to the house breaker panel to bury heavy-gauge electrical conduit. A decade later, at age seventy-two, I was up on the steep roof pitch of our Quail Creek house, single-handedly wiring an 8 kW array and hoisting 300-watt panels into the sky on a custom-fabricated battery winch.

When your lower back is throbbing after eight hours of screeding wet concrete, your knuckles are bleeding from barbed wire, or you're balancing on a ladder sixteen feet up trying to set a purlin straight, the woodshop feels like a vacation. 

True physical sovereignty isn't proven by how delicately you can sand a piece of cherry; it is proven when a multi-ton civil project demands every ounce of your physical strength, your mathematical calculations, and your endurance—and you stay on the job until the foundation is level and the concrete is cured.

* Core Heuristic & Persona Takeaway:
  * The Hierarchy of Effort (Civil vs. Domestic Craft): Do not confuse delicate indoor hobby craft with true heavy labor; real structural endurance is tested when the materials weigh tons, the weather is hostile, and the work must be finished before the chemical or physical clock runs out.
  * Mechanical Scale & Sunk Effort: Massive physical structures (concrete drives, 7,500 sq ft pole barns, perimeter fencing) require long-term project management, precise material estimation, and the mental stamina to work through multi-year build phases without surrendering momentum.
  * Autonomy Earned in the Dirt: You purchase genuine independence from commercial contractors not by reading manuals, but by picking up the shovel, setting the transit, running the winch, and mastering the physical loads yourself.

---
 
# Module 7: Global Expeditions & Wilderness Telemetry

---

### Story 7.1: The Great Alaska Wilderness & Inside Passage -- 25-Watt Cabins, Glacier Helicopters, and the 30-Foot Travel Trailer (2006)

* Search Triggers / Alternate Titles: Alaska trip 2006, Alaska interior with Bill and Doris Brookes, Holland America Statendam cruise 2006, Denali school bus tour, Copper River plank salmon, Kenai Peninsula Russian Orthodox priest, glacier helicopter hike, Valdez yacht cruise, Seward to Vancouver.
* Key People: Terri (wife & travel partner), Bill & Doris Brookes (Alaskan adventure hosts).
* Visual Artifact / Photo Link: ![Alaska Scene](https://i.postimg.cc/tRFF1DcC/Alaska-Scene.jpg) ![Alaska Bill & Doris](https://i.postimg.cc/VkJ6tkNW/Alaska-Bii-amp-Doris.jpg) ![Alaska Boobies](https://i.postimg.cc/C54ZcryR/Alaska-Boobies.jpg) ![Alaska Caribou](https://i.postimg.cc/htXjmtGD/Alaska-Caribu.jpg) ![Alaska Ice Field](https://i.postimg.cc/gc33w4T1/Alaska-Ice-field.jpg) ![Alaska Me and Terri](https://i.postimg.cc/VsXXS4hP/Alaska-Me-and-Terri.jpg) ![Alaska Moose](https://i.postimg.cc/9QDM9Qfc/Alaska-Moose-Personal.jpg) ![Alaska Pipeline](https://i.postimg.cc/6p83ZpQ6/Alaska-Pipline.jpg) ![Alaska Russian Orthodox Church](https://i.postimg.cc/htNG1RJH/Alaska-Russian-Orthodox-Church.jpg) ![Alaska Sea Lions](https://i.postimg.cc/NFxydChq/Alaska-Sea-Lions.jpg) ![Alaska Temp Home](https://i.postimg.cc/44ppKw0T/Alaska-Temp-home.jpg) ![Alaska Waterfall](https://i.postimg.cc/RVMJWmfv/Alaska-Waterfall.jpg) ![Valdez Tour Boat](https://i.postimg.cc/G2GTyhqQ/Valdez-Tour-Boat.jpg) ![Valdez Tour Into Glacier](https://i.postimg.cc/28zL1Yv7/Valdez-Tour-Into-Glacier-Parts.jpg)

In the summer of 2006, Terri and I set off on an extraordinary dual-stage adventure that paired a rugged overland journey through the Alaska interior with a seven-day southward cruise aboard Holland America's Statendam.

We flew out of Springfield through Seattle to Anchorage on Alaska Airlines, picked up a rental car, and pointed the compass north toward Fairbanks. Along the way, we spent a night near Denali in a spartan creekside cabin lit by a single 25-watt light bulb, with no indoor bathroom. We hiked up the hill to use the facilities and walked all the way to the top to a rustic hilltop restaurant. The chef invited me straight into the kitchen to watch her slice a thick filet from a fresh-caught Copper River king salmon, which she baked over an aromatic cedar plank--hands down the single greatest cut of fish I have ever tasted in my life.

The next day, we rode a rugged converted school bus into Denali National Park, navigating narrow mountain dirt shelves with sheer drops that were as breathtaking as they were terrifying.
